<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the president of the United States. For other people with the same name, see <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Zachary Taylor (disambiguation)">Zachary Taylor (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1784-11-24</span>)</span>November 24, 1784<br /><a href="/wiki/Barboursville,_Virginia" title="Barboursville, Virginia">Barboursville, Virginia</a>, U.S.</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">July 9, 1850<span style="display:none">(1850-07-09)</span> (aged&#160;65)<br /><a href="/wiki/Washington_County,_D.C." title="Washington County, D.C.">Washington County, D.C.</a>, U.S.</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor_National_Cemetery" title="Zachary Taylor National Cemetery">Zachary Taylor National Cemetery</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>

</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Zachary Taylor</b> (November 24, 1784 – July 9, 1850) was an American military leader who served as the 12th <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1849 until his death in 1850. Taylor was a career officer in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army" title="United States Army">United States Army</a>, rising to the rank of <a href="/wiki/Major_general_(United_States)" title="Major general (United States)">major general</a> and becoming a national hero for his victories in the <a href="/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War" title="Mexican–American War">Mexican–American War</a>. As a result, he won election to the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> despite his vague political beliefs. His top priority as president was to preserve the Union. He died 16 months into his term from a <a href="/wiki/Stomach_disease" title="Stomach disease">stomach disease</a>.
</p><p>Taylor was born into a prominent family of plantation owners who moved westward from <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a> to <a href="/wiki/Louisville,_Kentucky" title="Louisville, Kentucky">Louisville, Kentucky</a>, in his youth; he was the last president born before the adoption of <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States" title="Constitution of the United States">the Constitution</a>. He was commissioned as an officer in the U.S. Army in 1808 and made a name for himself as a <a href="/wiki/Captain_(United_States)" title="Captain (United States)">captain</a> in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>. He climbed the ranks of the military, establishing military forts along the <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_River" title="Mississippi River">Mississippi River</a> and entering the <a href="/wiki/Black_Hawk_War" title="Black Hawk War">Black Hawk War</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Colonel_(United_States)" title="Colonel (United States)">colonel</a> in 1832. His success in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Seminole_War" title="Second Seminole War">Second Seminole War</a> attracted national attention and earned him the nickname "Old Rough and Ready".
</p><p>In 1845, during the <a href="/wiki/Texas_annexation" title="Texas annexation">annexation of Texas</a>, President <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a> dispatched Taylor to the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Grande" title="Rio Grande">Rio Grande</a> in anticipation of a battle with <a href="/wiki/Centralist_Republic_of_Mexico" title="Centralist Republic of Mexico">Mexico</a> over the disputed <a href="/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border" title="Mexico–United States border">Texas–Mexico border</a>. The Mexican–American War broke out in April 1846, and Taylor defeated Mexican troops commanded by General <a href="/wiki/Mariano_Arista" title="Mariano Arista">Mariano Arista</a> at the battles of <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Palo_Alto" title="Battle of Palo Alto">Palo Alto</a> and <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Resaca_de_la_Palma" title="Battle of Resaca de la Palma">Resaca de la Palma</a>, driving Arista's troops out of <a href="/wiki/Texas" title="Texas">Texas</a>. Taylor then led his troops into Mexico, where they defeated Mexican troops commanded by <a href="/wiki/Pedro_de_Ampudia" title="Pedro de Ampudia">Pedro de Ampudia</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Monterrey" title="Battle of Monterrey">Battle of Monterrey</a>. Defying orders, Taylor led his troops further south and, despite being severely outnumbered, dealt a crushing blow to Mexican forces under General <a href="/wiki/Antonio_L%C3%B3pez_de_Santa_Anna" title="Antonio López de Santa Anna">Antonio López de Santa Anna</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Buena_Vista" title="Battle of Buena Vista">Battle of Buena Vista</a>. Taylor's troops were transferred to the command of Major General <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a>, but Taylor retained his popularity.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a> convinced a reluctant Taylor to lead its ticket in the <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 presidential election</a>, despite his unclear political tenets and lack of interest in politics. At the <a href="/wiki/1848_Whig_National_Convention" title="1848 Whig National Convention">1848 Whig National Convention</a>, Taylor defeated Winfield Scott and former senator <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> for the party's nomination. He won the general election alongside New York politician <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Millard Fillmore</a>, defeating Democratic Party nominees <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Orlando_Butler" title="William Orlando Butler">William Orlando Butler</a>, as well as a third-party effort led by former president <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a> and <a href="/wiki/Charles_Francis_Adams_Sr." title="Charles Francis Adams Sr.">Charles Francis Adams Sr.</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Free_Soil_Party" title="Free Soil Party">Free Soil Party</a>. Taylor became the first president to be elected without having previously held political office. As president, he kept his distance from <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">Congress</a> and his <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_States" title="Cabinet of the United States">Cabinet</a>, even though partisan tensions threatened to divide the Union. Debate over the status of slavery in the <a href="/wiki/Mexican_Cession" title="Mexican Cession">Mexican Cession</a> dominated the national political agenda and led to threats of <a href="/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States" title="Secession in the United States">secession</a> from Southerners. Despite being a Southerner and a slaveholder himself, Taylor did not push for the expansion of slavery, and sought sectional harmony above all other concerns. To avoid the issue of slavery, he urged settlers in <a href="/wiki/New_Mexico" title="New Mexico">New Mexico</a> and California to bypass the territorial stage and draft constitutions for <a href="/wiki/U.S._state" title="U.S. state">statehood</a>, setting the stage for the <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1850" title="Compromise of 1850">Compromise of 1850</a>.
</p><p>Taylor died suddenly of a stomach disease on July 9, 1850, with his administration having accomplished little aside from the ratification of the <a href="/wiki/Clayton%E2%80%93Bulwer_Treaty" title="Clayton–Bulwer Treaty">Clayton–Bulwer Treaty</a> and having made no progress on the most divisive issue in Congress and the nation: <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slavery</a>. Vice President Fillmore <a href="/wiki/United_States_presidential_line_of_succession" title="United States presidential line of succession">assumed the presidency</a> and served the remainder of his term. Historians and scholars have <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">ranked</a> Taylor in the bottom quartile of U.S. presidents, owing in part to his short term of office (16 months), though he has been described as "more a forgettable president than a failed one".<sup id="cite_ref-Tolson_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tolson-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG/250px-Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG/375px-Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG/500px-Zachary_Taylor_House_1.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Taylor's childhood home in <a href="/wiki/Louisville,_Kentucky" title="Louisville, Kentucky">Louisville, Kentucky</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Zachary Taylor was born on November 24, 1784,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> on a <a href="/wiki/Plantations_in_the_American_South" class="mw-redirect" title="Plantations in the American South">plantation</a> in <a href="/wiki/Orange_County,_Virginia" title="Orange County, Virginia">Orange County, Virginia</a>, to a prominent family of <a href="/wiki/Planter_class" title="Planter class">planters</a> of <a href="/wiki/English_people" title="English people">English</a> ancestry. His birthplace may have been <a href="/wiki/Hare_Forest_Farm" title="Hare Forest Farm">Hare Forest Farm</a>, the home of his maternal grandfather William Strother, but this is uncertain.<sup id="cite_ref-vaNRHPnom_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vaNRHPnom-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Another possibility, one recognized by a historical marker, is Montebello, another Orange County estate.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He was the third of five surviving sons in his family (a sixth died in infancy) and had three younger sisters. His mother was Sarah Dabney (Strother) Taylor. His father, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Taylor_(colonel)" title="Richard Taylor (colonel)">Richard Taylor</a>, served as a lieutenant colonel in the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolution" title="American Revolution">American Revolution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19851–2_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19851–2-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194121–24,_261–262_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194121–24,_261–262-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor was a descendant of Elder <a href="/wiki/William_Brewster_(Pilgrim)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Brewster (Pilgrim)">William Brewster</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Pilgrim_(Plymouth_Colony)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pilgrim (Plymouth Colony)">Pilgrim</a> leader of the <a href="/wiki/Plymouth_Colony" title="Plymouth Colony">Plymouth Colony</a>, a <i><a href="/wiki/Mayflower" title="Mayflower">Mayflower</a></i> immigrant, and a signer of the <a href="/wiki/Mayflower_Compact" title="Mayflower Compact">Mayflower Compact</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Allerton_Jr." title="Isaac Allerton Jr.">Isaac Allerton Jr.</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Colonial_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Colonial America">colonial</a> merchant, colonel, and son of <i>Mayflower</i> Pilgrim <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Allerton" title="Isaac Allerton">Isaac Allerton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fear_Brewster" title="Fear Brewster">Fear Brewster</a>. Taylor's second cousin through that line was <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a>, the fourth president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194122,_259_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194122,_259-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He was also a member of the famous Lee family of Virginia, and a third cousin once removed of <a href="/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America" title="Confederate States of America">Confederate</a> General <a href="/wiki/Robert_E._Lee" title="Robert E. Lee">Robert E. Lee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His family forsook its exhausted <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a> land, joined the westward migration and settled near future <a href="/wiki/Louisville,_Kentucky" title="Louisville, Kentucky">Louisville</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kentucky" title="Kentucky">Kentucky</a>, on the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_River" title="Ohio River">Ohio River</a>. Taylor grew up in a small woodland cabin until, with increased prosperity, his family moved to a brick house. As a child, he lived in a battleground of the <a href="/wiki/American_Indian_Wars" title="American Indian Wars">American Indian Wars</a>, later claiming that he had seen <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Native Americans</a> abduct and <a href="/wiki/Scalping" title="Scalping">scalp</a> his classmates while they were walking down the road together.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Louisville's rapid growth was a boon for Taylor's father, who by the start of the 19th century had acquired 10,000 acres (40&#160;km<sup>2</sup>) throughout Kentucky, as well as 26 slaves to cultivate the most developed portion of his holdings. Taylor's formal education was sporadic because Kentucky's education system was just taking shape during his formative years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19854_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19854-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor's mother taught him to read and write,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> and he later attended a school operated by Elisha Ayer, a teacher originally from Connecticut.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19854_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19854-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He also attended a <a href="/wiki/Middletown,_Kentucky" title="Middletown, Kentucky">Middletown, Kentucky</a>, academy run by Kean O'Hara, a classically trained scholar from <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a> and the father of <a href="/wiki/Theodore_O%27Hara" title="Theodore O&#39;Hara">Theodore O'Hara</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Ayer recalled Taylor as a patient and quick learner, but his early letters showed a weak grasp of spelling and grammar,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19852–4_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19852–4-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194125–29_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194125–29-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> as well as poor handwriting. All improved over time, but his handwriting remained difficult to read.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19852–4_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19852–4-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194125–29_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194125–29-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_family">Marriage and family</span></h2>
<p>In June 1810, Taylor married <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Taylor" title="Margaret Taylor">Margaret Mackall Smith</a>, whom he had met the previous autumn in Louisville. "Peggy" Smith came from a prominent family of <a href="/wiki/Maryland" title="Maryland">Maryland</a> planters—her father was Major Walter Smith, who had served in the Revolutionary War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19858–9_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19858–9-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194137_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194137-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The couple had six children:
</p>
<ul><li>Ann Mackall Taylor (1811–1875),<sup id="cite_ref-miller-facts_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller-facts-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> married Robert C. Wood, a U.S. Army surgeon at Fort Snelling, in 1829.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198548–49_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198548–49-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Their son <a href="/wiki/John_Taylor_Wood" title="John Taylor Wood">John Taylor Wood</a> served in the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Navy" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Navy">U.S. Navy</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Confederate_Navy" class="mw-redirect" title="Confederate Navy">Confederate Navy</a>. Wood was the father of:
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor_Wood" title="Zachary Taylor Wood">Zachary Taylor Wood</a>, acting Commissioner of the <a href="/wiki/North-West_Mounted_Police" title="North-West Mounted Police">North-West Mounted Police</a> and Commissioner of <a href="/wiki/Yukon_Territory" class="mw-redirect" title="Yukon Territory">Yukon Territory</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Carroll_Wood" title="Charles Carroll Wood">Charles Carroll Wood</a>, Lieutenant with the <a href="/wiki/British_Army" title="British Army">British Army</a>.</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sarah_Knox_Taylor" title="Sarah Knox Taylor">Sarah Knox "Knoxie" Taylor</a> (1814–1835),<sup id="cite_ref-miller-facts_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller-facts-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> married <a href="/wiki/Jefferson_Davis" title="Jefferson Davis">Jefferson Davis</a> in 1835, a subordinate officer she had met through her father at the end of the Black Hawk War; she died at 21 of <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a> in <a href="/wiki/St._Francisville,_Louisiana" title="St. Francisville, Louisiana">St. Francisville, Louisiana</a>, three months after her marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198569–70_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198569–70-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Octavia Pannell Taylor (1816–1820),<sup id="cite_ref-miller-facts_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller-facts-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> died in early childhood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198538_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198538-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Margaret Smith Taylor (1819–1820),<sup id="cite_ref-miller-facts_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller-facts-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> died in infancy along with Octavia when the Taylor family was stricken with a "bilious fever."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198538_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198538-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mary_Elizabeth_Bliss" title="Mary Elizabeth Bliss">Mary Elizabeth "Betty" Taylor</a> (1824–1909),<sup id="cite_ref-miller-facts_18-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller-facts-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> married <a href="/wiki/William_Wallace_Smith_Bliss" title="William Wallace Smith Bliss">William Wallace Smith Bliss</a> in 1848 (he died in 1853);<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985243_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985243-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> married Philip Pendleton Dandridge in 1858.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Richard_Taylor_(Confederate_general)" title="Richard Taylor (Confederate general)">Richard Taylor</a> (1826–1879),<sup id="cite_ref-miller-facts_18-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller-facts-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> a <a href="/wiki/Confederate_Army" class="mw-redirect" title="Confederate Army">Confederate Army</a> general;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008138–139_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008138–139-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> married Louise Marie Myrthe Bringier in 1851.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_career">Military career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Initial_commissions">Initial commissions</span></h3>
<p>On May 3, 1808, Taylor joined the U.S. Army, receiving a <a href="/wiki/Commissioned_officer" class="mw-redirect" title="Commissioned officer">commission</a> from President <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> as a <a href="/wiki/First_lieutenant" title="First lieutenant">first lieutenant</a> of the <a href="/wiki/7th_Infantry_Regiment_(United_States)" title="7th Infantry Regiment (United States)">Kentuckian Seventh Infantry Regiment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> He was among the new officers Congress commissioned in response to the <a href="/wiki/Chesapeake%E2%80%93Leopard_affair" title="Chesapeake–Leopard affair"><i>Chesapeake–Leopard</i> affair</a>, in which the crew of a British <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> warship had boarded a <a href="/wiki/United_States_Navy" title="United States Navy">United States Navy</a> frigate, sparking calls for war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19855_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19855-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194133_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194133-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Taylor spent much of 1809 in the dilapidated camps of <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a> and nearby <a href="/wiki/St._Bernard_Parish,_Louisiana" title="St. Bernard Parish, Louisiana">Terre aux Boeufs</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Territory_of_Orleans" title="Territory of Orleans">Territory of Orleans</a>. Under <a href="/wiki/James_Wilkinson" title="James Wilkinson">James Wilkinson</a>'s command, the soldiers at Terre aux Boeufs suffered greatly from disease and lack of supplies, and Taylor was given an extended leave, returning to Louisville to recover.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower20084–6_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower20084–6-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor was promoted to <a href="/wiki/Captain_(United_States)" title="Captain (United States)">captain</a> in November 1810. His army duties were limited at this time, and he attended to his personal finances. Over the next several years, he began to purchase a good deal of bank stock in Louisville.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer19855–10_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer19855–10-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194135–37_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194135–37-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> He also bought a <a href="/wiki/Plantations_in_the_American_South" class="mw-redirect" title="Plantations in the American South">plantation</a> in Louisville, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Cypress_Grove_Plantation" title="Cypress Grove Plantation">Cypress Grove Plantation</a> in <a href="/wiki/Jefferson_County,_Mississippi" title="Jefferson County, Mississippi">Jefferson County</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_Territory" title="Mississippi Territory">Mississippi Territory</a>. These acquisitions included slaves, rising in number to more than 200.<sup id="cite_ref-ANTI-SLAVERY_REPORTER_VOL_III_1848,_p_194-5_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ANTI-SLAVERY_REPORTER_VOL_III_1848,_p_194-5-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1811 he was called to the <a href="/wiki/Indiana_Territory" title="Indiana Territory">Indiana Territory</a>, where he assumed control of <a href="/wiki/Fort_Knox_(Vincennes)" class="mw-redirect" title="Fort Knox (Vincennes)">Fort Knox</a> after the commandant fled. In a few weeks, he was able to restore order in the garrison, for which he was lauded by Governor <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198510_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198510-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194137–38_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194137–38-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Taylor was temporarily called to Washington to testify for Wilkinson as a witness in a <a href="/wiki/Court-martial" title="Court-martial">court-martial</a>, and so did not take part in the November 1811 <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tippecanoe" title="Battle of Tippecanoe">Battle of Tippecanoe</a> against the forces of <a href="/wiki/Tecumseh" title="Tecumseh">Tecumseh</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Shawnee" title="Shawnee">Shawnee</a> <a href="/wiki/Tribal_chief" title="Tribal chief">chief</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower20087–8_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower20087–8-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_of_1812">War of 1812</span></h3>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>, in which U.S. forces battled the British Empire and its <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Indian</a> allies, Taylor defended <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Fort_Harrison" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Fort Harrison">Fort Harrison</a> in Indiana Territory from an Indian attack commanded by Tecumseh. The September 1812 battle was the American forces' first land victory of the war, for which Taylor received wide praise, as well as a <a href="/wiki/Brevet_(military)" title="Brevet (military)">brevet</a> (temporary) promotion to the rank of major. According to historian <a href="/wiki/John_Eisenhower" title="John Eisenhower">John Eisenhower</a>, this was the first brevet awarded in U.S. history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200810–11_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200810–11-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Later that year, Taylor joined General <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Hopkins_(congressman)" title="Samuel Hopkins (congressman)">Samuel Hopkins</a> as an aide on two expeditions—one into the <a href="/wiki/Illinois_Territory" title="Illinois Territory">Illinois Territory</a> and one to the Tippecanoe battle site, where they were forced to retreat in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Wild_Cat_Creek" title="Battle of Wild Cat Creek">Battle of Wild Cat Creek</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198513–19_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198513–19-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194139–46_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194139–46-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Taylor moved his family to Fort Knox after the violence subsided.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198520–24_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198520–24-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194147–53_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194147–53-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the spring of 1814, Taylor was called back into action under Brigadier General <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Howard_(Missouri_politician)" title="Benjamin Howard (Missouri politician)">Benjamin Howard</a>, and after Howard fell sick, Taylor led a 430-man expedition from <a href="/wiki/St._Louis" title="St. Louis">St. Louis</a>, up the Mississippi River. In the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Credit_Island" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Credit Island">Battle of Credit Island</a>, Taylor defeated Indian forces, but retreated after the Indians were joined by their British allies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200813–15_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200813–15-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> That October he supervised the construction of <a href="/wiki/Fort_Johnson" title="Fort Johnson">Fort Johnson</a> near present-day <a href="/wiki/Warsaw,_Illinois" title="Warsaw, Illinois">Warsaw, Illinois</a>, the last toehold of the U.S. Army in the upper Mississippi River Valley. Upon Howard's death a few weeks later, Taylor was ordered to abandon the fort and retreat to St. Louis. Reduced to the rank of captain when the war ended in 1815, he resigned from the army. He reentered it a year later after gaining a commission as a major.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198520–30_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198520–30-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194147–59_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194147–59-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Command_of_Fort_Howard">Command of Fort Howard</span></h3>
<p>Taylor commanded <a href="/wiki/Fort_Howard_(Wisconsin)" title="Fort Howard (Wisconsin)">Fort Howard</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin" title="Green Bay, Wisconsin">Green Bay</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michigan_Territory" title="Michigan Territory">Michigan Territory</a> settlement for two years, then returned to Louisville and his family. In April 1819 he was promoted to the rank of lieutenant colonel and dined with President <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">James Monroe</a> and General <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200817–19_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200817–19-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In late 1820, Taylor took the 7th Infantry to <a href="/wiki/Natchitoches,_Louisiana" title="Natchitoches, Louisiana">Natchitoches</a>, <a href="/wiki/Louisiana" title="Louisiana">Louisiana</a>, on the <a href="/wiki/Red_River_of_the_South" title="Red River of the South">Red River</a>. He subsequently established <a href="/wiki/Fort_Selden_(Louisiana)" title="Fort Selden (Louisiana)">Fort Selden</a> at the confluence of the <a href="/wiki/Sulphur_River" title="Sulphur River">Sulphur River</a> and the Red River. On the orders of General <a href="/wiki/Edmund_P._Gaines" title="Edmund P. Gaines">Edmund P. Gaines</a>, he later found a new post more convenient to the Sabine River frontier. By March 1822, Taylor had established <a href="/wiki/Fort_Jesup" title="Fort Jesup">Fort Jesup</a> at the Shield's Spring site southwest of Natchitoches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198540–41_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198540–41-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>That November (1822), Taylor was transferred to <a href="/wiki/Pentagon_Barracks" title="Pentagon Barracks">Baton Rouge</a><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> on the <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_River" title="Mississippi River">Mississippi River</a> in <a href="/wiki/Louisiana" title="Louisiana">Louisiana</a>, where he remained until February 1824.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198540–47_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198540–47-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194170–77_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194170–77-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He spent the next few years on recruiting duty. In late 1826, he was called to <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, for work on an Army committee to consolidate and improve military organization. In the meantime he acquired his first Louisiana plantation and decided to move with his family to a new home in <a href="/wiki/Baton_Rouge,_Louisiana" title="Baton Rouge, Louisiana">Baton Rouge</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198540–47_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198540–47-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194170–77_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194170–77-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Black_Hawk_War">Black Hawk War</span></h3>
<p>In May 1828, Taylor was called back to action, commanding <a href="/wiki/Fort_Snelling" title="Fort Snelling">Fort Snelling</a> in <a href="/wiki/Michigan_Territory" title="Michigan Territory">Michigan Territory</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Minnesota" title="Minnesota">Minnesota</a>) on the <a href="/wiki/Upper_Mississippi_River" title="Upper Mississippi River">Upper Mississippi River</a> for a year, and then nearby <a href="/wiki/Fort_Crawford" title="Fort Crawford">Fort Crawford</a> for a year. After some time on furlough, spent expanding his landholdings, Taylor was promoted to colonel of the <a href="/wiki/1st_Infantry_Regiment_(United_States)" title="1st Infantry Regiment (United States)">1st Infantry Regiment</a> in April 1832, when the <a href="/wiki/Black_Hawk_War" title="Black Hawk War">Black Hawk War</a> was beginning in the West.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198547–49_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198547–49-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194177–82_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194177–82-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Taylor campaigned under General <a href="/wiki/Henry_Atkinson_(soldier)" title="Henry Atkinson (soldier)">Henry Atkinson</a> to pursue and later defend against Chief <a href="/wiki/Black_Hawk_(Sauk_leader)" title="Black Hawk (Sauk leader)">Black Hawk</a>'s forces throughout the summer. The end of the war in August 1832 signaled the final Indian resistance to U.S. expansion in the area.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198559–65_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198559–65-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194183–99_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194183–99-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During this period, Taylor opposed the courtship of his 17-year-old daughter Sarah Knox Taylor with Lieutenant Jefferson Davis, the future <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Confederate_States_of_America" title="President of the Confederate States of America">President of the Confederate States of America</a>. He respected Davis but did not wish his daughter to become a military wife, as he knew it was a hard life for families. Davis and Sarah Taylor married in June 1835 (when she was 21), but she died three months later of <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a> contracted on a visit to Davis's sister's home in <a href="/wiki/St._Francisville,_Louisiana" title="St. Francisville, Louisiana">St. Francisville, Louisiana</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198559–74_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198559–74-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton194183–109_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton194183–109-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Seminole_War">Second Seminole War</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg/220px-Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="286" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg/330px-Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg/440px-Florida_war_1837_-_en.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1153" data-file-height="1500" /></a><figcaption>Florida war 1837</figcaption></figure>
<p>By 1837, the <a href="/wiki/Second_Seminole_War" title="Second Seminole War">Second Seminole War</a> was underway when Taylor was directed to Florida. He built <a href="/wiki/Fort_Gardiner" title="Fort Gardiner">Fort Gardiner</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fort_Basinger_(Seminole_War_Fort)" title="Fort Basinger (Seminole War Fort)">Fort Basinger</a> as supply depots and communication centers in support of Major General <a href="/wiki/Thomas_S._Jesup" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas S. Jesup">Thomas S. Jesup</a>’s campaign to penetrate deep into Seminole territory with large forces and trap the Seminoles and their allies in order to force them to fight or surrender. He engaged in battle with the <a href="/wiki/Seminole_Indians" class="mw-redirect" title="Seminole Indians">Seminole Indians</a> in the Christmas Day <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lake_Okeechobee" title="Battle of Lake Okeechobee">Battle of Lake Okeechobee</a>, among the largest U.S.–Indian battles of the 19th century; as a result, he was promoted to brigadier general. In May 1838, Jesup stepped down and placed Taylor in command of all American troops in Florida, a position he held for two years—his reputation as a military leader was growing and he became known as "Old Rough and Ready."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198575–95_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198575–95-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941122–141_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941122–141-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Taylor was criticized for using <a href="/wiki/Bloodhound" title="Bloodhound">bloodhounds</a> in order to track Seminole.<sup id="cite_ref-ANTI-SLAVERY_REPORTER_VOL_III_1848,_p_194-5_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ANTI-SLAVERY_REPORTER_VOL_III_1848,_p_194-5-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After his long-requested relief was granted, Taylor spent a comfortable year touring the nation with his family and meeting with military leaders. During this period, he began to be interested in politics and corresponded with President <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a>. He was made commander of the Second Department of the Army's Western Division in May 1841. The sizable territory ran from the Mississippi River westward, south of the <a href="/wiki/37th_parallel_north" title="37th parallel north">37th parallel north</a>. Stationed in <a href="/wiki/Arkansas" title="Arkansas">Arkansas</a>, Taylor enjoyed several uneventful years, spending as much time attending to his land speculation as to military matters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer198596–110_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer198596–110-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941142–155_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941142–155-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Mexican.E2.80.93American_War"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mexican–American_War">Mexican–American War</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War" title="Mexican–American War">Mexican–American War</a></div>
<p>In anticipation of the annexation of the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Texas" title="Republic of Texas">Republic of Texas</a>, which had established independence in 1836, Taylor was sent in April 1844 to <a href="/wiki/Fort_Jesup" title="Fort Jesup">Fort Jesup</a> in Louisiana, and ordered to guard against attempts by Mexico to reclaim the territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985111_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985111-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941156–158_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941156–158-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> More senior generals in the army might have taken this important command, such as <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edmund_P._Gaines" title="Edmund P. Gaines">Edmund P. Gaines</a>. But both were known members of the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>, and Taylor's apolitical reputation and friendly relations with Andrew Jackson made him the choice of President <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200830–31_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200830–31-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Polk directed him to deploy into disputed territory in Texas, "on or near the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Grande" title="Rio Grande">Rio Grande</a>" near Mexico. Taylor chose a spot at <a href="/wiki/Corpus_Christi,_Texas" title="Corpus Christi, Texas">Corpus Christi</a>, and his <a href="/wiki/Army_of_Occupation_(Mexico)" title="Army of Occupation (Mexico)">Army of Occupation</a> encamped there until the following spring in anticipation of a Mexican attack.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985116–123_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985116–123-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941158–165_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941158–165-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8,_1846.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8%2C_1846.JPG/220px-General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8%2C_1846.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8%2C_1846.JPG/330px-General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8%2C_1846.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8%2C_1846.JPG/440px-General_Zachary_Taylor_rides_his_horse_at_Palo_Alto_Battle_-_May_8%2C_1846.JPG 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="598" /></a><figcaption>General Zachary Taylor rides his horse at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Palo_Alto" title="Battle of Palo Alto">Battle of Palo Alto</a>, May 8, 1846</figcaption></figure>
<p>When Polk's attempts to negotiate with Mexico failed, Taylor's men advanced to the Rio Grande in March 1846, and war appeared imminent. Violence broke out several weeks later, when Mexican forces attacked some of Captain Seth B. Thornton's men north of the river.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985123–129,_145–149_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985123–129,_145–149-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941170–177_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941170–177-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Learning of the <a href="/wiki/Thornton_Affair" title="Thornton Affair">Thornton Affair</a>, Polk told Congress in May that a war between Mexico and the U.S. had begun.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985166_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985166-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941195_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941195-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>That same month, Taylor commanded American forces at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Palo_Alto" title="Battle of Palo Alto">Battle of Palo Alto</a> and the nearby <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Resaca_de_la_Palma" title="Battle of Resaca de la Palma">Battle of Resaca de la Palma</a>. Though greatly outnumbered, he defeated the Mexican "<a href="/wiki/Army_of_the_North_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Army of the North (disambiguation)">Army of the North</a>" commanded by General <a href="/wiki/Mariano_Arista" title="Mariano Arista">Mariano Arista</a>, and forced the troops back across the Rio Grande.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985152–162_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985152–162-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941181–190_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941181–190-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Taylor was later praised for his humane treatment of the wounded Mexican soldiers before the prisoner exchange with Arista, giving them the same care as was given to American wounded. After tending to the wounded, he performed the last rites for the dead of both the American and Mexican soldiers killed during the battle.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>These victories made him a popular hero, and in May 1846 Taylor received a brevet promotion to major general and a formal commendation from Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFry2009186–187_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFry2009186–187-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> In June, he was promoted to the full rank of major general.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFry2009188_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFry2009188-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The national press compared him to <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a> and Andrew Jackson, both generals who had ascended to the presidency, but Taylor denied any interest in running for office. "Such an idea never entered my head," he remarked in a letter, "nor is it likely to enter the head of any sane person."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941198–199_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941198–199-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After crossing the Rio Grande, in September Taylor inflicted heavy casualties upon the Mexicans at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Monterrey" title="Battle of Monterrey">Battle of Monterrey</a>, and captured that city in three days, despite its impregnable repute. Taylor was criticized for signing a "liberal" truce rather than pressing for a large-scale surrender.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985166–185_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985166–185-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941207–216_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941207–216-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Polk had hoped that the occupation of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Mexico" title="Northern Mexico">Northern Mexico</a> would induce the Mexicans to sell <a href="/wiki/Alta_California" title="Alta California">Alta California</a> and <a href="/wiki/Santa_Fe_de_Nuevo_M%C3%A9xico" title="Santa Fe de Nuevo México">New Mexico</a>, but the Mexicans remained unwilling to part with so much territory. Polk sent an army under the command of <a href="/wiki/Winfield_Scott" title="Winfield Scott">Winfield Scott</a> to <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Veracruz" title="Siege of Veracruz">besiege Veracruz</a>, an important Mexican port city, while Taylor was ordered to remain near Monterrey. Many of Taylor's experienced soldiers were placed under Scott's command, leaving Taylor with a smaller and less effective force. Mexican General <a href="/wiki/Antonio_L%C3%B3pez_de_Santa_Anna" title="Antonio López de Santa Anna">Antonio López de Santa Anna</a> intercepted a letter from Scott about Taylor's smaller force, and he moved north, intent on destroying Taylor's force before confronting Scott's army.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200862–66_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200862–66-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Learning of Santa Anna's approach, and refusing to retreat despite the Mexican army's greater numbers, Taylor established a strong defensive position near the town of <a href="/wiki/Saltillo" title="Saltillo">Saltillo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200866–68_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200866–68-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Santa Anna attacked Taylor with 20,000 men at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Buena_Vista" title="Battle of Buena Vista">Battle of Buena Vista</a> in February 1847, leaving around 700 Americans dead or wounded at a cost of over 1,500 Mexican casualties.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Outmatched, the Mexican forces retreated, ensuring a "far-reaching" victory for the Americans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985186-207-2_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985186-207-2-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941217–242_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941217–242-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In recognition of his victory at Buena Vista, on July 4, 1847, Taylor was elected an honorary member of the New York <a href="/wiki/Society_of_the_Cincinnati" title="Society of the Cincinnati">Society of the Cincinnati</a>, the Virginia branch of which included his father as a charter member. Taylor also was made a member of the <a href="/wiki/Aztec_Club_of_1847" title="Aztec Club of 1847">Aztec Club of 1847</a>, Military Society of the Mexican War.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> He received three <a href="/wiki/Congressional_Gold_Medal" title="Congressional Gold Medal">Congressional Gold Medals</a> for his service in the Mexican-American War and remains the only person to have received the medal three times.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ZacharyTaylorMonmouthBalize.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/ZacharyTaylorMonmouthBalize.jpg/220px-ZacharyTaylorMonmouthBalize.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/ZacharyTaylorMonmouthBalize.jpg/330px-ZacharyTaylorMonmouthBalize.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d0/ZacharyTaylorMonmouthBalize.jpg 2x" data-file-width="347" data-file-height="277" /></a><figcaption>U.S. Steam Ship <i>Monmouth</i> returns U.S. General Zachary Taylor from victories in the war with Mexico at <a href="/wiki/La_Balize,_Louisiana" title="La Balize, Louisiana">Balize, Louisiana</a>, November 1847</figcaption></figure>
<p>Taylor remained at Monterrey until late November 1847, when he set sail for home. While he spent the following year in command of the Army's entire western division, his active military career was effectively over. In December he received a hero's welcome in New Orleans and Baton Rouge, setting the stage for the 1848 presidential election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941248–255_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941248–255-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant" title="Ulysses S. Grant">Ulysses S. Grant</a> served under Taylor in this war and said of his style of leadership: "A better army, man for man, probably never faced an enemy than the one commanded by General Taylor in the earliest two engagements of the Mexican War."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith201183_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith201183-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>General Taylor was not an officer to trouble the administration much with his demands but was inclined to do the best he could with the means given to him. He felt his responsibility as going no further. If he had thought that he was sent to perform an impossibility with the means given him, he would probably have informed the authorities of his opinion and left them to determine what should be done. If the judgment was against him he would have gone on and done the best he could with the means at hand without parading his grievance before the public. No soldier could face either danger or responsibility more calmly than he. These are qualities more rarely found than genius or physical courage. General Taylor never made any great show or parade, either of uniform or retinue. In dress he was possibly too plain, rarely wearing anything in the field to indicate his rank, or even that he was an officer; but he was known to every soldier in his army, and was respected by all.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1848">Election of 1848</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1848_United_States_presidential_election" title="1848 United States presidential election">1848 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TaylorFillmore.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/TaylorFillmore.jpg/250px-TaylorFillmore.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/TaylorFillmore.jpg/375px-TaylorFillmore.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/TaylorFillmore.jpg/500px-TaylorFillmore.jpg 2x" data-file-width="617" data-file-height="439" /></a><figcaption>Taylor/Fillmore 1848 campaign poster</figcaption></figure>
<p>In his capacity as a career officer, Taylor had never publicly revealed his political beliefs before 1848 nor voted before that time.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> He was <a href="/wiki/Apolitical" class="mw-redirect" title="Apolitical">apolitical</a> and had a negative opinion of most politicians. He thought of himself as an <a href="/wiki/Independent_(voter)" class="mw-redirect" title="Independent (voter)">independent</a>, believing in a strong and sound banking system for the country, and thought that President <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> should not have allowed the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> to collapse in 1836.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> He believed it was impractical to expand slavery into the <a href="/wiki/Western_United_States" title="Western United States">Western United States</a>, as neither cotton nor sugar (both produced in great quantities as a result of slavery) could be easily grown there through a <a href="/wiki/Plantation_economy" title="Plantation economy">plantation economy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> He was also a firm <a href="/wiki/American_nationalism" title="American nationalism">American nationalist</a>, and due to his experience of seeing many people die as a result of warfare, believed that <a href="/wiki/Secession" title="Secession">secession</a> was a bad way to resolve national problems.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Well before the American victory at Buena Vista, political clubs formed that supported Taylor for president. His support was drawn from an unusually broad assortment of political bands, including <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whigs</a> and Democrats, Northerners and Southerners, allies and opponents of national leaders such as Polk and Henry Clay. By late 1846, Taylor's opposition to a presidential run had weakened, and it became clear that his principles resembled Whig orthodoxy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton195138–44_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton195138–44-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Taylor despised both Polk and his policies, while the Whigs were considering nominating another war hero for the presidency after the success of its previous winning nominee, <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a>, in 1840.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECohen201952_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen201952-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As support for Taylor's candidacy grew, he continued to keep his distance from both parties, but made clear that he would have voted for Whig <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a> in 1844 had he voted. In a widely publicized September 1847 letter, Taylor stated his positions on several issues. He did not favor chartering another national bank, favored a low <a href="/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariffs in United States history">tariff</a>, and believed that the president should play no role in making laws. Taylor did believe that the president could veto laws, but only when they were clearly unconstitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith198840–42_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith198840–42-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many southerners believed that Taylor supported slavery and its expansion into the new territory absorbed from Mexico, and some were angered when Taylor suggested that if elected president he would not veto the <a href="/wiki/Wilmot_Proviso" title="Wilmot Proviso">Wilmot Proviso</a>, which proposed against such an expansion.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> This position did not enhance his support from activist antislavery elements in the <a href="/wiki/Northern_United_States" title="Northern United States">Northern United States</a>, as they wanted Taylor to speak out strongly in support of the Proviso, not simply fail to veto it.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Most <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolitionists</a> did not support Taylor, since he was a slave-owner.<sup id="cite_ref-MCCAE_90-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCCAE-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ElectoralCollege1848.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/ElectoralCollege1848.svg/250px-ElectoralCollege1848.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/ElectoralCollege1848.svg/375px-ElectoralCollege1848.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/ElectoralCollege1848.svg/500px-ElectoralCollege1848.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1020" data-file-height="593" /></a><figcaption>1848 electoral vote results</figcaption></figure>
<p>In February 1848, Taylor again announced that he would not accept either party's presidential nomination. His reluctance to identify himself as a Whig nearly cost him the party's presidential nomination, but Senator <a href="/wiki/John_J._Crittenden" title="John J. Crittenden">John J. Crittenden</a> of Kentucky and other supporters finally convinced Taylor to declare himself a Whig.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith198840–42_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith198840–42-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Though Clay retained a strong following among the Whigs, Whig leaders like <a href="/wiki/William_H._Seward" title="William H. Seward">William H. Seward</a> and <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> were eager to support a war hero who could replicate the success of the party's only other successful presidential candidate, William Henry Harrison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith198820–21_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith198820–21-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1848_Whig_National_Convention" title="1848 Whig National Convention">1848 Whig National Convention</a>, Taylor defeated Clay and Winfield Scott for the presidential nomination. For its vice-presidential nominee the convention chose <a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Millard Fillmore</a>, a prominent <a href="/wiki/New_York_(state)" title="New York (state)">New York</a> Whig who had chaired the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_Ways_and_Means" title="United States House Committee on Ways and Means">House Ways and Means Committee</a> and been a contender for the vice-presidential nominee in the <a href="/wiki/1844_United_States_presidential_election" title="1844 United States presidential election">1844 election</a>. Fillmore's selection was largely an attempt at reconciliation with northern Whigs, who were furious at the nomination of a slave-owning southerner; no faction of the party was satisfied with the final ticket.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985236–238_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985236–238-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton195194–97_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton195194–97-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> It was initially unclear whether Taylor would accept the nomination because he did not respond to the letters notifying him of the convention's outcome, because he had instructed his local post office not to deliver his mail to avoid postage fees.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Taylor continued to minimize his role in the campaign, preferring not to directly meet with voters or correspond about his political views. He did little active campaigning, and may not have voted.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> His campaign was skillfully directed by Crittenden and bolstered by a late endorsement from Senator <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Webster" title="Daniel Webster">Daniel Webster</a> of Massachusetts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985239–244_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985239–244-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Democrats were even less unified than the Whigs, as former Democratic President <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a> broke from the party and led the anti-slavery <a href="/wiki/Free_Soil_Party" title="Free Soil Party">Free Soil Party</a>'s ticket. Van Buren won the support of many Democrats and Whigs who opposed the extension of slavery into the territories, but he took more votes from Democratic nominee <a href="/wiki/Lewis_Cass" title="Lewis Cass">Lewis Cass</a> in the crucial state of New York.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith198821–23_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith198821–23-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nationally, Taylor defeated Cass and Van Buren, taking 163 of the 290 <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">electoral votes</a>. In the popular vote, he took 47.3%, to Cass's 42.5% and Van Buren's 10.1%. Taylor was the last Whig to be elected president and the last person elected to the presidency from neither the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a> nor the <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republican Party</a>, as well as the last Southerner to win a presidential election until <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson" title="Woodrow Wilson">Woodrow Wilson</a>'s election in <a href="/wiki/1912_United_States_presidential_election" title="1912 United States presidential election">1912</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor ignored the Whig platform, as historian Michael F. Holt explains:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Taylor was equally indifferent to programs Whigs had long considered vital. Publicly, he was artfully ambiguous, refusing to answer questions about his views on banking, the tariff, and internal improvements. Privately, he was more forthright. The idea of a national bank "is dead, and will not be revived in my time." In the future the tariff "will be increased only for revenue"; in other words, Whig hopes of restoring the protective <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1842" title="Tariff of 1842">tariff of 1842</a> were vain. There would never again be surplus federal funds from public land sales to distribute to the states, and internal improvements "will go on in spite of presidential vetoes." In a few words, that is, Taylor pronounced an epitaph for the entire Whig economic program.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281849.E2.80.931850.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1849–1850)">Presidency (1849–1850)<span class="anchor" id="Presidency"></span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Taylor administration" redirects here. For the Liberian government led by President Charles G. Taylor, see <a href="/wiki/Charles_G._Taylor#Presidency" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles G. Taylor">Charles G. Taylor §&#160;Presidency</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox" style="padding-bottom:2px; padding-top:2px;"><tbody><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image" style="border-bottom:0;
