</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="text-align:center;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:United_States_presidential_transitions_series" title="Template:United States presidential transitions series"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:United_States_presidential_transitions_series" title="Template talk:United States presidential transitions series"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:United_States_presidential_transitions_series&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>As president-elect, Taylor kept his distance from Washington, not resigning his Western Division command until late January 1849. He spent the months following the election formulating his cabinet selections. He was deliberate and quiet about his decisions, to the frustration of his fellow Whigs. While he despised <a href="/wiki/Patronage" title="Patronage">patronage</a> and political games, he endured a flurry of advances from office-seekers looking to play a role in his administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985248–251_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985248–251-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While he would appoint no Democrats, Taylor wanted his cabinet to reflect the nation's diverse interests, and so apportioned the seats geographically. He also avoided choosing prominent Whigs, sidestepping such obvious selections as Clay. He saw Crittenden as a cornerstone of his administration, offering him the crucial seat of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a>, but Crittenden insisted on serving out the governorship of Kentucky to which he had just been elected. Taylor settled on Senator <a href="/wiki/John_M._Clayton" title="John M. Clayton">John M. Clayton</a> of Delaware, a close associate of Crittenden's.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985248–251_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985248–251-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<table class="infobox" style="width:auto;text-align:left;line-height:1.2em;margin-left:1em; margin-right:0; float:right; clear:right;"><tbody><tr><th colspan="3" style="line-height:1.5em;font-size:110%;background:#DCDCDC;text-align:center">The Taylor cabinet</th></tr><tr><th>Office</th><th>Name</th><th>Term</th></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#000"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">President</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold">Zachary Taylor</th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/Vice_President_of_the_United_States" title="Vice President of the United States">Vice President</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Millard_Fillmore" title="Millard Fillmore">Millard Fillmore</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/John_M._Clayton" title="John M. Clayton">John M. Clayton</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/William_M._Meredith" title="William M. Meredith">William M. Meredith</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/George_W._Crawford" title="George W. Crawford">George W. Crawford</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Attorney_General" title="United States Attorney General">Attorney General</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Reverdy_Johnson" title="Reverdy Johnson">Reverdy Johnson</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General" title="United States Postmaster General">Postmaster General</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Jacob_Collamer" title="Jacob Collamer">Jacob Collamer</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/William_Ballard_Preston" title="William Ballard Preston">William Ballard Preston</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3" style="background:#D1D1D1"></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Interior" title="United States Secretary of the Interior">Secretary of the Interior</a></td><th style="font-weight:bold"><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Ewing" title="Thomas Ewing">Thomas Ewing</a></th><td>1849&#8211;1850</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>With Clayton's aid, Taylor chose the six remaining members of his cabinet. One of the incoming Congress's first actions would be to establish the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_the_Interior" title="United States Department of the Interior">Department of the Interior</a>, so Taylor would be appointing that department's inaugural secretary. <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Ewing" title="Thomas Ewing">Thomas Ewing</a>, who had previously served as a senator from Ohio and as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Secretary of the Treasury">Secretary of the Treasury</a> under William Henry Harrison, accepted the patronage-rich position of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Interior" title="United States Secretary of the Interior">Secretary of the Interior</a>. For the position of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General" title="United States Postmaster General">Postmaster General</a>, also a center of patronage, Taylor chose Congressman <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Collamer" title="Jacob Collamer">Jacob Collamer</a> of Vermont.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200890–94,_128_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200890–94,_128-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After <a href="/wiki/Horace_Binney" title="Horace Binney">Horace Binney</a> refused appointment as Secretary of the Treasury, Taylor chose another prominent Philadelphian, <a href="/wiki/William_M._Meredith" title="William M. Meredith">William M. Meredith</a>. <a href="/wiki/George_W._Crawford" title="George W. Crawford">George W. Crawford</a>, a former governor of Georgia, accepted the position of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a>, while Congressman <a href="/wiki/William_Ballard_Preston" title="William Ballard Preston">William B. Preston</a> of Virginia became <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_the_Navy" title="United States Secretary of the Navy">Secretary of the Navy</a>. Senator <a href="/wiki/Reverdy_Johnson" title="Reverdy Johnson">Reverdy Johnson</a> of Maryland accepted appointment as <a href="/wiki/United_States_Attorney_General" title="United States Attorney General">Attorney General</a>, and became one of the most influential members of Taylor's cabinet. Fillmore was not in favor with Taylor, and was largely sidelined throughout Taylor's presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200890–94,_128_103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200890–94,_128-103">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor began his trek to Washington in late January, a journey rife with bad weather, delays, injuries, sickness—and an <a href="/wiki/Ashland,_Jefferson_County,_Mississippi#Deception_of_President_Taylor" title="Ashland, Jefferson County, Mississippi">abduction</a> by a family friend. Taylor finally arrived in the nation's capital on February 24 and soon met with the outgoing President Polk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985251–253_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985251–253-104">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Polk held a low opinion of Taylor, privately deeming him "without political information" and "wholly unqualified for the station" of president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985247–248_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985247–248-105">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Taylor spent the next week meeting with political elites, some of whom were unimpressed with his appearance and demeanor. With less than two weeks until his inauguration, he met with Clayton and hastily finalized his cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985253–255,_260–262_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985253–255,_260–262-106">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Inauguration">Inauguration</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Inauguration_of_Zachary_Taylor" title="Inauguration of Zachary Taylor">Inauguration of Zachary Taylor</a></div>
<p>Taylor's term as president began on Sunday, March 4, but his inauguration was not held until the next day out of religious concerns.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> His inauguration speech discussed the many tasks facing the nation, but presented a governing style of deference to Congress and sectional compromise instead of assertive executive action.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985256–258_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985256–258-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> His speech also emphasized the importance of following President Washington's <a href="/wiki/Washington_Doctrine_of_Unstable_Alliances" title="Washington Doctrine of Unstable Alliances">precedent in avoiding entangling alliances</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200894–95_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200894–95-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the period after his inauguration, Taylor made time to meet with numerous office-seekers and other ordinary citizens who desired his attention. He also attended an unusual number of funerals, including services for Polk and <a href="/wiki/Dolley_Madison" title="Dolley Madison">Dolley Madison</a>. According to Eisenhower, Taylor coined the phrase "<a href="/wiki/First_Lady_of_the_United_States" title="First Lady of the United States">First Lady</a>" in his eulogy for Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200896–97_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower200896–97-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> In the summer of 1849, Taylor toured the <a href="/wiki/Northeastern_United_States" title="Northeastern United States">Northeastern United States</a> to familiarize himself with a region of which he had seen little. He spent much of the trip plagued by gastrointestinal illness and returned to Washington by September.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985268–270_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985268–270-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sectional_crisis">Sectional crisis</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg/180px-Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="233" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg/270px-Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg/360px-Zachary_Taylor_12th_US_President.jpg 2x" data-file-width="928" data-file-height="1202" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Daguerreotype" title="Daguerreotype">Daguerreotype</a> of Taylor by <a href="/wiki/Mathew_Brady" title="Mathew Brady">Mathew Brady</a>, 1849<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>As Taylor took office, Congress faced a battery of questions related to the <a href="/wiki/Mexican_Cession" title="Mexican Cession">Mexican Cession</a>, acquired by the U.S. after the Mexican War and divided into military districts. It was unclear which districts would be established into states and which would become federal territories, while the question of their slave status threatened to bitterly divide Congress. Southerners objected to the admission of the <a href="/wiki/History_of_California" title="History of California">California Territory</a>, the <a href="/wiki/New_Mexico_Territory" title="New Mexico Territory">New Mexico Territory</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Utah_Territory" title="Utah Territory">Utah Territory</a> to the Union as <a href="/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states" title="Slave states and free states">free states</a> despite California's demographic and economic growth. Additionally, Southerners had grown increasingly angry about the aid that Northerners had given to <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_slaves_in_the_United_States" title="Fugitive slaves in the United States">fugitive slaves</a> after <i><a href="/wiki/Prigg_v._Pennsylvania" title="Prigg v. Pennsylvania">Prigg v. Pennsylvania</a></i> allowed <a href="/wiki/Slave_catcher" title="Slave catcher">slave catchers</a> to capture alleged <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_slaves_in_the_United_States" title="Fugitive slaves in the United States">runaway slaves</a> in free states, and that Northern authorities frequently refused to enforce the <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1793" title="Fugitive Slave Act of 1793">Fugitive Slave Act of 1793</a>. At the same time, northerners demanded the abolition of the <a href="/wiki/Domestic_slave_trade" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic slave trade">domestic slave trade</a> in <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington DC</a>. Finally, Texas claimed parts of eastern New Mexico and threatened to send its <a href="/wiki/Texas_Militia" title="Texas Militia">state militia</a> to militarily enforce its territorial claims.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008101–102_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008101–102-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While a Southern slaveowner himself, Taylor believed that slavery was economically infeasible in the Mexican Cession, and so opposed slavery in those territories as a needless source of controversy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008101–102_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008101–102-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> His major goal was sectional peace, preserving the Union through legislative compromise.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985289–290_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985289–290-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> As the threat of Southern secession grew, he sided increasingly with antislavery Northerners such as Senator <a href="/wiki/William_H._Seward" title="William H. Seward">William H. Seward</a> of New York, even suggesting that he would sign the <a href="/wiki/Wilmot_Proviso" title="Wilmot Proviso">Wilmot Proviso</a> to ban slavery in federal territories should such a bill reach his desk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985295–298_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985295–298-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Taylor's view, the best way forward was to admit California as a state rather than a federal territory, as it would leave the slavery question out of Congress's hands. The timing for statehood was in Taylor's favor, as the <a href="/wiki/California_Gold_Rush" title="California Gold Rush">California Gold Rush</a> was well underway at the time of his inauguration, and California's population was exploding.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985290–291_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985290–291-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> The administration dispatched Representative <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Butler_King" title="Thomas Butler King">Thomas Butler King</a> to California, to test the waters and advocate statehood, knowing that Californians were certain to adopt an anti-slavery constitution. King found that a <a href="/wiki/California_Constitutional_Conventions" title="California Constitutional Conventions">constitutional convention</a> was already underway, and by October 1849, the convention unanimously agreed to join the Union—and to ban slavery within their borders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985291–292_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985291–292-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:United_States_1849-1850.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/United_States_1849-1850.png/300px-United_States_1849-1850.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="203" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/United_States_1849-1850.png/450px-United_States_1849-1850.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/United_States_1849-1850.png/600px-United_States_1849-1850.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="677" /></a><figcaption>United States states (Texas border unsettled, California admitted in 1850) and territories during Taylor's presidency</figcaption></figure>
<p>The question of the New Mexico–Texas border was unsettled at the time of Taylor's inauguration. The territory newly won from Mexico was under federal jurisdiction, but the Texans claimed a swath of land north of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Fe,_New_Mexico" title="Santa Fe, New Mexico">Santa Fe</a> and were determined to include it within their borders, despite having no significant presence there. Taylor sided with the New Mexicans' claim, initially pushing to keep it as a federal territory, but eventually supported statehood so as to further reduce the slavery debate in Congress. The Texas government, under newly instated governor <a href="/wiki/P._Hansborough_Bell" class="mw-redirect" title="P. Hansborough Bell">P. Hansborough Bell</a>, tried to ramp up military action in defense of the territory against the federal government, but was unsuccessful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985292–294_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985292–294-120">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Latter_Day_Saint" class="mw-redirect" title="Latter Day Saint">Latter Day Saint</a> (Mormon) settlers of modern-day Utah had established a provisional <a href="/wiki/State_of_Deseret" title="State of Deseret">State of Deseret</a>, an enormous swath of territory that had little hope of recognition by Congress. The Taylor administration considered combining the California and Utah territories but instead opted to organize the <a href="/wiki/Utah_Territory" title="Utah Territory">Utah Territory</a>. To alleviate the Mormon concerns over religious freedom, Taylor promised they would have relative independence from Congress despite being a federal territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985294_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985294-121">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor sent his only <a href="/wiki/State_of_the_Union_address" class="mw-redirect" title="State of the Union address">State of the Union report</a> to Congress in December 1849. He recapped international events and suggested several adjustments to tariff policy and executive organization, but the sectional crisis facing Congress overshadowed such issues. He reported on California's and New Mexico's applications for statehood, and recommended that Congress approve them as written and "should abstain from the introduction of those exciting topics of a sectional character".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985298–299_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985298–299-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> The policy report was prosaic and unemotional, but ended with a sharp condemnation of secessionists. It had no effect on Southern legislators, who saw the admission of two free states as an existential threat, and Congress remained stalled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985299–300_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985299–300-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/History_of_U.S._foreign_policy,_1829%E2%80%931861" title="History of U.S. foreign policy, 1829–1861">History of U.S. foreign policy, 1829–1861</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg/330px-President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg" decoding="async" width="330" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg/495px-President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg/660px-President_Taylor_and_his_cabinet.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1491" data-file-height="937" /></a><figcaption>President Zachary Taylor standing in front of his Cabinet, seated from left: <a href="/wiki/Reverdy_Johnson" title="Reverdy Johnson">Reverdy Johnson</a>, Attorney General; <a href="/wiki/William_M._Meredith" title="William M. Meredith">William M. Meredith</a>, Secretary of the Treasury; <a href="/wiki/William_B._Preston" class="mw-redirect" title="William B. Preston">William B. Preston</a>, Secretary of the Navy; <a href="/wiki/George_W._Crawford" title="George W. Crawford">George W. Crawford</a>, Secretary of War; <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Collamer" title="Jacob Collamer">Jacob Collamer</a>, Postmaster General; <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Ewing" title="Thomas Ewing">Thomas Ewing</a>, Secretary of the Interior; and <a href="/wiki/John_M._Clayton" title="John M. Clayton">John M. Clayton</a>, Secretary of State. <a href="/wiki/Lithograph" class="mw-redirect" title="Lithograph">Lithograph</a> by Francis D'Avignon, published by <a href="/wiki/Mathew_Brady" title="Mathew Brady">Mathew Brady</a>, 1849.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Taylor and his Secretary of State, <a href="/wiki/John_M._Clayton" title="John M. Clayton">John M. Clayton</a>, both lacked diplomatic experience and came into office at a relatively uneventful time in American–international politics. Their shared nationalism allowed Taylor to devolve foreign policy matters to Clayton with minimal oversight, although no decisive foreign policy was established under their administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985273–274,_288_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985273–274,_288-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> As opponents of the autocratic European order, they vocally supported <a href="/wiki/German_revolutions_of_1848%E2%80%931849" title="German revolutions of 1848–1849">German</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1848" title="Hungarian Revolution of 1848">Hungarian liberals</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Revolutions_of_1848" title="Revolutions of 1848">revolutions of 1848</a>, although they offered nothing in the way of aid.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985274–275_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985274–275-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A perceived insult from the French minister Guillaume Tell Poussin nearly led to a break in diplomatic relations until Poussin was replaced, and a <a href="/wiki/Reparation_(legal)" title="Reparation (legal)">reparation</a> dispute with Portugal resulted in harsh words from the Taylor administration. In a more positive effort, the administration arranged for two ships to assist in the United Kingdom's search for a team of British explorers, led by <a href="/wiki/John_Franklin" title="John Franklin">John Franklin</a>, who had gotten lost in the Arctic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985275–278_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985275–278-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> While previous Whig administrations had emphasized Pacific trade as an economic imperative, the Taylor administration took no major initiative in the Far East.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985287–288_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985287–288-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout 1849 and 1850, they contended with <a href="/wiki/Narciso_L%C3%B3pez" title="Narciso López">Narciso López</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Venezuela" title="Venezuela">Venezuelan</a> radical who led repeated <a href="/wiki/Filibuster_(military)" title="Filibuster (military)">filibustering expeditions</a> in an attempt to conquer the Spanish <a href="/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba" title="Captaincy General of Cuba">Captaincy General of Cuba</a>. The annexation of Cuba was the object of fascination among many in the South, who saw in Cuba a potential new slave state, and López had several prominent Southern supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008113–114_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008113–114-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> López made generous offers to <a href="/wiki/United_States_Armed_Forces" title="United States Armed Forces">United States Armed Forces</a> leaders to support him, but Taylor and Clayton saw the enterprise as illegal. They issued a blockade, and later, authorized a mass arrest of López and his fellows, although the group would eventually be acquitted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985278–280_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985278–280-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> They also confronted Spain, which had arrested several Americans on the charge of piracy, but the Spaniards eventually surrendered them to maintain good relations with the U.S.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985280–281_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985280–281-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Arguably the Taylor administration's definitive accomplishment in foreign policy was the <a href="/wiki/Clayton%E2%80%93Bulwer_Treaty" title="Clayton–Bulwer Treaty">Clayton–Bulwer Treaty</a> of 1850, regarding a proposed inter-oceanic canal through <a href="/wiki/Central_America" title="Central America">Central America</a>. While the U.S. and Britain were on friendly terms, and the construction of such a canal was decades away from reality, the mere possibility put the two nations in an uneasy position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985281_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985281-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> For several years, Britain had been seizing strategic points, particularly the <a href="/wiki/Mosquito_Coast" title="Mosquito Coast">Mosquito Coast</a> on the eastern coast of present-day <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua" title="Nicaragua">Nicaragua</a>. Negotiations were held with Britain that resulted in the landmark Clayton–Bulwer Treaty. Both nations agreed not to claim control of any canal that might be built in Nicaragua. The treaty promoted the development of an Anglo-American alliance; its completion was Taylor's last action as president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985281–287_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985281–287-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Compromise_attempts_and_final_days">Compromise attempts and final days</span></h3>
<p>Clay took a central role as Congress debated slavery. While his positions overlapped somewhat with Taylor's, the president always maintained his distance from Clay. Historians disagree on his motivations for doing so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301,_307–308_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301,_307–308-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> This caused Taylor to become politically isolated as Southerners disapproved of his preference to appoint the territories of the Mexican Cession as free states while Northerners disapproved of his opposition to Clay's legislative agenda. As a result, Congress increasingly ignored Taylor while drafting a compromise.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECohen201969_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen201969-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> With assistance from Daniel Webster, Clay developed his landmark proposal, the <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1850" title="Compromise of 1850">Compromise of 1850</a>. The proposal allowed statehood for California, giving it independence on the slavery question, while the other territories would remain under federal jurisdiction. This included the disputed parts of New Mexico, although Texas would be reimbursed for the territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Slavery would be retained in the District of Columbia, but the slave trade would be banned. Meanwhile, a strict <a href="/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Law" class="mw-redirect" title="Fugitive Slave Law">Fugitive Slave Law</a> would be enacted, bypassing northern legislation which had restricted Southerners from retrieving runaway slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301_135-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tensions flared as Congress negotiated and secession talks grew, culminating with a threat from Taylor to send troops into New Mexico to protect its border from Texas, with himself leading the army. The crisis escalated after delegates in New Mexico proposed a new state constitution that would have banned slavery and <a href="/wiki/Peter_Hansborough_Bell" title="Peter Hansborough Bell">Peter Hansborough Bell</a> won the <a href="/wiki/1849_Texas_gubernatorial_election" title="1849 Texas gubernatorial election">1849 Texas gubernatorial election</a> on a pledge to order a militia invasion of New Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Southern senators accused Taylor of secretly sending the U.S. Army to New Mexico; Taylor denied the allegations but emphasized that he would like to. He also said that anyone "taken in rebellion against the Union, he would hang ... with less reluctance than he had hanged deserters and spies in Mexico."<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The omnibus law was a major step forward on these issues but ultimately could not pass, due to extremists on both sides and Taylor's opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301–312_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985301–312-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> At this point, Taylor began to receive disapproval from even his own political allies. Secretary of War Crawford warned Taylor he would not approve a military deployment to New Mexico, although Taylor said he would give the order himself. Taylor's close advisors <a href="/wiki/Robert_Toombs" title="Robert Toombs">Robert Toombs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alexander_H._Stephens" title="Alexander H. Stephens">Alexander Stephens</a>, both <a href="/wiki/Southern_Whig_Party_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Southern Whig Party (United States)">Southern Whigs</a> who later served as officials in the Confederate government, warned him that his rhetoric on New Mexico would drive Southerners out of the party.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_136-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>No great compromise reached Taylor's desk during his presidency; instead, his last days were overshadowed by the <a href="/wiki/Galphin_affair" class="mw-redirect" title="Galphin affair">Galphin affair</a>. Before joining the Taylor cabinet, Crawford had served as a lawyer. He had been involved in a 15-year case, representing the descendants of a colonial trader whose services to the British crown had not been repaid at the time of the American Revolution. The British debt to George Galphin was to be assumed by the federal government, but Galphin's heirs received payment on the debt's principal only after years of litigation, and were unable to win an interest payment from the Polk administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985312–313_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985312–313-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Treasury Secretary Meredith, with the support of Attorney General Johnson, finally signed off on the payment in April 1850. To Taylor's embarrassment, the payment included a legal compensation of nearly $100,000 to Crawford; two cabinet members had effectively offered a tremendous chunk of the public treasury to another. A House investigation cleared Crawford of any legal wrongdoing, but nonetheless expressed disapproval of his accepting the payment. Taylor, who had already been sketching a reorganization of his cabinet, now had an unfolding scandal to complicate the situation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985312–313_139-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985312–313-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> The House of Representatives voted to censure Taylor, and newspapers of both parties began calling for his impeachment.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor,_12th_President_of_the_United_States_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor%2C_12th_President_of_the_United_States_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor%2C_12th_President_of_the_United_States_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor%2C_12th_President_of_the_United_States_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor%2C_12th_President_of_the_United_States_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor%2C_12th_President_of_the_United_States_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Death_of_Genl._Z._Taylor%2C_12th_President_of_the_United_States_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1344" data-file-height="1008" /></a><figcaption>An 1850 print depicting the death of Zachary Taylor</figcaption></figure>
<p>On July 4, 1850, Taylor reportedly consumed copious amounts of cherries and iced milk while attending holiday celebrations during a fund-raising event at the <a href="/wiki/Washington_Monument" title="Washington Monument">Washington Monument</a>, which was then under construction.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985314_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985314-144">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hamilton_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hamilton-145">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Over the course of several days, he became severely ill with an unknown digestive ailment initially resembling acute <a href="/wiki/Gastroenteritis" title="Gastroenteritis">gastroenteritis</a>. The illness initially seemed mild, and on the first day Taylor felt well enough to continue working. His condition worsened thereafter. His Army physician Alexander S. Wotherspoon "diagnosed the illness as <a href="/wiki/Cholera_morbus" class="mw-redirect" title="Cholera morbus">cholera morbus</a>, a flexible mid-nineteenth-century term for intestinal ailments as diverse as <a href="/wiki/Diarrhea" title="Diarrhea">diarrhea</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dysentery" title="Dysentery">dysentery</a> but not related to Asiatic <a href="/wiki/Cholera" title="Cholera">cholera</a>", the latter a <a href="/wiki/1846%E2%80%931860_cholera_pandemic" title="1846–1860 cholera pandemic">widespread epidemic</a> prevalent in Washington DC at the time of Taylor's death.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_140-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985314–316_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985314–316-147">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> The identity and source of Taylor's illness are the subject of historical speculation <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033199720">.mw-parser-output div.crossreference{padding-left:0}</style><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><span role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable crossreference selfref">(see <a href="#Assassination_theories">below</a>)</span>, although it is known that several of his cabinet members had come down with a similar illness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008133_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008133-148">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Fever ensued and Taylor's chance of recovery was small. On July 8, Taylor remarked to a medical attendant:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I should not be surprised if this were to terminate in my death. I did not expect to encounter what has beset me since my elevation to the Presidency. God knows I have endeavored to fulfill what I conceived to be an honest duty. But I have been mistaken. My motives have been misconstrued, and my feelings most grossly outraged.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p></blockquote>
<p>Taylor died at 10:35&#160;p.m. on July 9, 1850. He was 65 years old.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985316_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985316-150">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> After his death, Vice President Fillmore assumed the presidency and completed Taylor's term, which ended on March 4, 1853. Soon after taking office, Fillmore signed into law the <a href="/wiki/Compromise_of_1850" title="Compromise of 1850">Compromise of 1850</a>, with the aim of settling many of the issues the Taylor administration faced.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008139–140_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEisenhower2008139–140-151">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A Joint Special Committee was appointed by the <a href="/wiki/New_York_City_Council" title="New York City Council">Common Council of the city of New York</a> to make the arrangements for Taylor's funeral, which took place in New York City on July 23, 1850. A procession moved from the Park and proceeded down Broadway, to Chatham Street to the <a href="/wiki/Bowery" title="Bowery">Bowery</a>; down to <a href="/wiki/Union_Square,_Manhattan" title="Union Square, Manhattan">Union Square</a>; and then in front of the <a href="/wiki/New_York_City_Hall" title="New York City Hall">City Hall</a>. The procession included the firing of three volleys by the 7th National Guard Regiment. There were 30 pallbearers, which was the number of states in the Union at that time.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Taylor was buried in an airtight <a href="/wiki/Fisk_metallic_burial_case" title="Fisk metallic burial case">Fisk metallic burial case</a> with a glass window plate for viewing the deceased's face.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> He was interred in the <a href="/wiki/Public_Vault_at_the_Congressional_Cemetery" title="Public Vault at the Congressional Cemetery">Public Vault</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Congressional_Cemetery" title="Congressional Cemetery">Congressional Cemetery</a> in Washington, D.C., from July 13 to October 25, 1850.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup> His body was transported to the Taylor family plot, where his parents were buried, on the old Taylor homestead plantation known as "Springfield" in Louisville, Kentucky.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Assassination_theories">Assassination theories</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TAYLOR,_Zachary-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/TAYLOR%2C_Zachary-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/180px-TAYLOR%2C_Zachary-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="248" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/TAYLOR%2C_Zachary-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/270px-TAYLOR%2C_Zachary-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/TAYLOR%2C_Zachary-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/360px-TAYLOR%2C_Zachary-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6110" data-file-height="8434" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Engraving_and_Printing" title="Bureau of Engraving and Printing">B.E.P.</a> engraved portrait</figcaption></figure>
<p>Almost immediately after his death, rumors began to circulate that Taylor had been poisoned by pro-slavery Southerners or <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholics</a>, and similar theories persisted into the 21st century.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> A few weeks after Taylor's death, President Fillmore received a letter alleging that Taylor had been poisoned by a <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Jesuit</a> lay official.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-158">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> In 1978, Hamilton Smith based his assassination theory on the timing of drugs, the lack of confirmed cholera outbreaks, and other material.<sup id="cite_ref-Hamilton_145-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hamilton-145">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Theories that Taylor had been murdered grew after the <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_Abraham_Lincoln" title="Assassination of Abraham Lincoln">assassination of Abraham Lincoln</a> in 1865. In 1881, <a href="/wiki/John_Bingham" title="John Bingham">John Bingham</a>, well known for serving as the Judge Advocate General at the Lincoln assassination trial, wrote an editorial in <i>The New York Times</i> alleging that <a href="/wiki/Jefferson_Davis" title="Jefferson Davis">Jefferson Davis</a> had poisoned Taylor.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_158-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-158">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> In the late 1980s, Clara Rising, a former professor at the University of Florida, persuaded Taylor's closest living relative to agree to an exhumation so that his remains could be tested.<sup id="cite_ref-orlando_july2593_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-orlando_july2593-159">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The remains were exhumed and transported to the Office of the <a href="/wiki/Kentucky" title="Kentucky">Kentucky</a> Chief Medical Examiner on June 17, 1991. Samples of hair, fingernail, and other tissues were removed, and radiological studies were conducted. The remains were returned to the cemetery and reinterred, with appropriate honors, in the mausoleum.<sup id="cite_ref-Marriott_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marriott-160">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Neutron_activation_analysis" title="Neutron activation analysis">Neutron activation analysis</a> conducted at <a href="/wiki/Oak_Ridge_National_Laboratory" title="Oak Ridge National Laboratory">Oak Ridge National Laboratory</a> revealed no evidence of poisoning, as <a href="/wiki/Arsenic" title="Arsenic">arsenic</a> levels were too low.<sup id="cite_ref-Marriott_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marriott-160">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ORNL_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ORNL-161">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The analysis concluded Taylor had contracted "cholera morbus, or acute gastroenteritis", as Washington had open sewers, and his food or drink may have been contaminated. Any potential for recovery was overwhelmed by his doctors, who treated him with "<a href="/wiki/Syrup_of_ipecac" title="Syrup of ipecac">ipecac</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mercury(I)_chloride" title="Mercury(I) chloride">calomel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Opium" title="Opium">opium</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Quinine" title="Quinine">quinine</a>" at 40 grains per dose (approximately 2.6 grams), and "bled and blistered him too."<sup id="cite_ref-Sampas_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sampas-162">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Political scientist <a href="/wiki/Michael_Parenti" title="Michael Parenti">Michael Parenti</a> questions the traditional explanation for Taylor's death. Relying on interviews and reports by forensic pathologists, he argues that the procedure used to test for arsenic poisoning was flawed.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> A 2010 review concludes: "there is no definitive proof that Taylor was assassinated, nor would it appear that there is definitive proof that he was not."<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation_and_memorials">Historical reputation and memorials</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG/220px-Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG/330px-Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG/440px-Zachary_Taylor_Grave.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Taylor's mausoleum at the <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor_National_Cemetery" title="Zachary Taylor National Cemetery">Zachary Taylor National Cemetery</a> in Louisville, Kentucky</figcaption></figure>
<p>Because of his short tenure, Taylor is not considered to have strongly influenced the office of the presidency or the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-MCIL_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCIL-166">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Some historians believe that he was too inexperienced with politics at a time when officials needed close ties with political operatives.<sup id="cite_ref-MCIL_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCIL-166">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Despite his shortcomings, the Clayton–Bulwer Treaty affecting relations with Great Britain in Central America is "recognized as an important step in scaling down the nation's commitment to <a href="/wiki/Manifest_Destiny" class="mw-redirect" title="Manifest Destiny">Manifest Destiny</a> as a policy."<sup id="cite_ref-MCIL_166-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MCIL-166">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">Historical rankings of presidents of the United States</a> have generally placed Taylor in the bottom quarter.<sup id="cite_ref-Tolson_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tolson-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Taylor was the last president to <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">own slaves</a> while in office. He was the third of four Whig presidents,<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;h&#93;</a></sup> the last being Fillmore, his successor. Taylor was also the second president to die in office, preceded by <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a>, who died while serving as president nine years earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1883, the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Kentucky" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth of Kentucky">Commonwealth of Kentucky</a> placed a 50-foot monument topped by a life-sized statue of Taylor near his grave. By the 1920s, the Taylor family initiated the effort to turn the Taylor burial grounds into a national cemetery. The Commonwealth of Kentucky donated two adjacent parcels of land for the project, turning the half-acre Taylor family cemetery into 16 acres (65,000&#160;m<sup>2</sup>). On May 5, 1926, the remains of Taylor and his wife (who died in 1852) were moved to the newly constructed Taylor mausoleum, made of limestone with a granite base and marble interior, nearby. The cemetery property was designated as the <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor_National_Cemetery" title="Zachary Taylor National Cemetery">Zachary Taylor National Cemetery</a> by <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a> <a href="/wiki/Dwight_F._Davis" title="Dwight F. Davis">Dwight F. Davis</a> on March 12, 1928.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/United_States_Post_Office_Department" title="United States Post Office Department">US Post Office</a> released <a href="/wiki/US_Presidents_on_US_postage_stamps" class="mw-redirect" title="US Presidents on US postage stamps">the first postage stamp issue</a> honoring Taylor on June 21, 1875, 25 years after his death. In 1938, Taylor again appeared on a US postage stamp, this time the 12-cent <a href="/wiki/Presidential_Issue" title="Presidential Issue">Presidential Issue of 1938</a>. His last appearance (to date, 2010) on a US postage stamp occurred in 1986, when he was honored on the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Presidents_on_U.S._postage_stamps#AMERIPEX_issues_of_1986" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Presidents on U.S. postage stamps">AMERIPEX presidential issue</a>. After Washington, Jefferson, Jackson and Lincoln, Taylor was the fifth American president to appear on US postage.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti class="thumbcaption">Postage stamp, issue of 1875</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:113px;max-width:113px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:111px;overflow:hidden"><span //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Zachary_Taylor_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg/222px-Zachary_Taylor_Presidential_%241_Coin_obverse.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1093" data-file-height="1093" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Presidential_$1_Coin_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="Presidential $1 Coin Program">Presidential dollar coin</a>, 2009</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>Taylor is the namesake of several entities and places around the nation, including:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Camp_Taylor,_Louisville" title="Camp Taylor, Louisville">Camp Taylor</a> in Kentucky and <a href="/wiki/Fort_Zachary_Taylor_Historic_State_Park" title="Fort Zachary Taylor Historic State Park">Fort Zachary Taylor</a> in Florida<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/SS_Zachary_Taylor" title="SS Zachary Taylor">SS <i>Zachary Taylor</i></a>, a World War II <a href="/wiki/Liberty_ship" title="Liberty ship">Liberty ship</a></li>
<li>Zachary Taylor Parkway in Louisiana<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> and Zachary Taylor Hall at <a href="/wiki/Southeastern_Louisiana_University" title="Southeastern Louisiana University">Southeastern Louisiana University</a><sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Taylor_County,_Georgia" title="Taylor County, Georgia">Taylor County, Georgia</a><sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Taylor_County,_Iowa" title="Taylor County, Iowa">Taylor County, Iowa</a><sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Taylor_County,_Kentucky" title="Taylor County, Kentucky">Taylor County, Kentucky</a><sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Rough_and_Ready,_California" title="Rough and Ready, California">Rough and Ready, California</a>; the historical origin of the town is depicted in a 1965 episode of the <a href="/wiki/Television_syndication" class="mw-redirect" title="Television syndication">syndicated</a> <a href="/wiki/Western_(genre)" title="Western (genre)">western</a> <a href="/wiki/Television_series" class="mw-redirect" title="Television series">television series</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Death_Valley_Days" title="Death Valley Days">Death Valley Days</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/U.S._Route_522_in_Virginia" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Route 522 in Virginia">Zachary Taylor Highway</a> in Virginia</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Taylor,_Michigan" title="Taylor, Michigan">Taylor, Michigan</a><sup id="cite_ref-taylor_michigan_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-taylor_michigan-179">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Taylor_Street" title="Taylor Street">Taylor Street</a>, Savannah, Georgia.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Taylor was also the namesake of architect <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Taylor_Davis" title="Zachary Taylor Davis">Zachary Taylor Davis</a>.
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Taylor's term of service was scheduled to begin at noon EST on March 4, 1849, but as this day fell on a Sunday, Taylor refused to be sworn in until the following day. Vice President Millard Fillmore was also not sworn in on that day. Most legal scholars agree that, according to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">Constitution</a>, Taylor's term began on March 4, regardless of whether he had taken the oath.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-82"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-82">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Estimates of casualties vary widely.<sup id="cite_ref-eb-buenavista_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-eb-buenavista-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Encyclopædia Britannica</i> lists casualties of about 1,500 Mexican to 700 American.<sup id="cite_ref-eb-buenavista_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-eb-buenavista-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Hamilton lists the "killed or wounded" as 673 Americans to "at least eighteen hundred" Mexicans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941241_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamilton1941241-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Bauer lists "594 killed, 1039 wounded, and 1,854 missing" on the Mexican side, with "272 killed, 387 wounded, and 6 missing" on the American side.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauer1985205–206_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauer1985205–206-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-100"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-100">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Taylor was not the last Whig to serve as president, nor was he the last Southerner to serve as president prior to Woodrow Wilson. Taylor was succeeded in office by Fillmore, who was also a member of the Whig Party. <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Johnson" title="Andrew Johnson">Andrew Johnson</a>, a Southerner, served as president from 1865 to 1869. However, neither Fillmore nor Johnson were directly elected to the presidency.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-108"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-108">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Folklore holds that <a href="/wiki/David_Rice_Atchison" title="David Rice Atchison">David Rice Atchison</a>, as <i>president pro tempore</i> of the Senate, unknowingly succeeded to the presidency for this day, but no major sources accept this view.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-142"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-142">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Recess_appointment" title="Recess appointment">Recess appointment</a>; formally nominated on December 21, 1849, confirmed by the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a> on August 2, 1850, and received commission on August 2, 1850.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-143"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-143">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Recess appointment; formally nominated on December 21, 1849, confirmed by the United States Senate on June 10, 1850, and received commission on June 10, 1850.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-155"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-155">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The Public Vault was built in 1835 to hold the remains of notables until either the grave site could be prepared, or transportation could be arranged to another city.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-167"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-167">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This numbering includes <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a>, who served as vice president under the Whig <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> but was expelled from his party shortly after becoming president.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
